U.S. federal references support selected terminology; their rules do not automatically govern every local, state, tribal, or international office.
Dialogue 07: Records request
Original fictional setting: An officer receives a broad public-records request.
Professional roles: Records officer / Department manager
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Records officer: The request covers emails about the project. Should staff delete duplicates before collecting them?
Department manager: Follow the records-preservation and review process. Don't alter the source records to simplify the response.
Records officer: I'll clarify the search scope and identify likely custodians.
Department manager: Route questions about exemptions and disclosure to the authorized reviewers.
Records officer: I'll record search methods and any locations we still need to check.
Department manager: Keep the requester informed through the applicable process without promising disclosure before review.
END REFERENCE
Dialogue 08: Budget justification
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Government and Public Administration English
Professional audience: public administrators, agency program managers, policy analysts, procurement staff, constituent-service teams, grants managers, and interagency coordinators
Scope: All programs, residents, offices, applications, figures, meetings, and local procedures are fictional. This is professional-English instruction, not legal advice or authority to decide eligibility, award contracts, release records, or spend funds. U.S. federal references support selected terminology; their rules do not automatically govern every local, state, tribal, or international office.
Dialogue 08: Budget justification
Original fictional setting: A manager prepares a budget request.
Professional roles: Program manager / Budget analyst
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Program manager: We want ten percent more funding because demand rose. Is the case strong enough?
Budget analyst: Show demand, service capacity, outcomes, and the cost of the proposed response on a consistent basis.
Program manager: Our applications increased, but completed cases didn't. The queue grew instead.
Budget analyst: Then explain that bottleneck and compare options, including recurring costs.
Program manager: I'll separate the additional staffing request from the one-time system expense.
Budget analyst: Include measurable objectives so the decision group can assess the proposal and later review its results.
END REFERENCE
Create more scenario dialogues — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Extend the conversation library
1. Propose six distinct, common scenarios in this field beyond the supplied case or script. For each, name the setting, two or more professional roles, the communication problem and one useful terminology focus. Include routine coordination, clarification, a complication, disagreement, handoff and follow-up where relevant. Choose scenarios that fit this profession, rather than forcing unsuitable situations into it.
2. Ask me to choose one scenario, or request all six in sequence. Stop and wait. Do not write all the scripts before I choose.
3. For the selected scenario, write an original fictional dialogue of 12-18 substantial speaking turns between two or three professionals. Name each role, establish an actual work problem, and let the exchange progress through questions, clarification, competing constraints and a credible next step or explicitly unresolved issue. Use the occupation's natural nomenclature and register. Avoid an interview between a teacher and a learner, generic small talk, and inserting a glossary definition into every reply. Explain specialist terms outside the dialogue instead.
4. After the script, explain five useful expressions in context and identify two grammar or register choices. Give three four-choice questions about the speakers' reasoning, with exactly one answer supported by the dialogue. Wait for my selections, then explain why each answer fits and why its alternatives fail. Add a role-switch reading using the supplied script; do not ask for an essay or invented written reply.
5. Before presenting a script, check names, numbers, chronology, roles and terminology for consistency. Label invented facts and acknowledge uncertain specialist usage. If I requested all six, deliver one complete script at a time and wait for "next". Make each scenario materially different.

REFERENCE
Course: Government and Public Administration English
Professional audience: public administrators, agency program managers, policy analysts, procurement staff, constituent-service teams, grants managers, and interagency coordinators
Scope: All programs, residents, offices, applications, figures, meetings, and local procedures are fictional. This is professional-English instruction, not legal advice or authority to decide eligibility, award contracts, release records, or spend funds. U.S. federal references support selected terminology; their rules do not automatically govern every local, state, tribal, or international office.
Lesson 01: Policy Implementation and Program Rules
Original fictional case: The fictional Brookfield Training Grant currently requires 12 months of residence, plus other conditions. Published guidance G4 explains the current requirements. A separate proposal P2 would reduce the residence period to six months, but it has not been adopted and has no effective date. Resident Amina has lived in the area for nine months and brings P2 to the service desk. Program officer Theo must identify the current residence requirement, explain the proposal accurately, and avoid either promising an award or treating this conversation as a formal denial of an application.
Language workshop: The proposal has not changed the current application test
Communication goal: Explain document status and a current requirement without confusing a preliminary check with a formal eligibility decision.
Language guidance: A proposal may be public without being in effect. A publication date does not necessarily establish adoption or applicability. Here G4 describes the current condition, while P2 remains an unadopted proposal with no effective date.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I can see why these two documents are easy to confuse. / G4 explains the requirements that currently apply. / P2 is a proposed change, not an adopted rule. / The current residence requirement is twelve months. / The proposal would reduce that period to six months. / No effective date has been established for P2. / Nine months is below the current residence requirement. / This explanation is not a formal eligibility determination. / I cannot promise an award based on the proposal. / The program also has other conditions to assess. / Which document and version are you reading? / I can show you the current application and review steps. / We should not invent an exception that the rules do not provide. / A comment on the proposal is not a grant application. / The official notice explains how to submit comments. / Use G4 for the current requirements and P2 for the proposed change.
Vocabulary: program rule: A requirement governing a defined public program. / eligibility criterion: A condition used to decide whether an applicant qualifies. / current guidance: Published explanatory material reflecting the presently applicable position. / proposal: A suggested change that has not necessarily been adopted. / draft guidance: Preliminary explanatory material not yet issued as the final version. / adoption: Formal acceptance of a proposed measure through the applicable process. / effective date: The date a measure begins to apply under its terms. / implementation date: The planned or required start of operational delivery. / transition provision: A rule addressing movement from an old arrangement to a new one. / residence requirement: A condition concerning where or how long a person has lived. / application intake: Receipt and initial handling of an application. / completeness check: A check that required application information is present. / eligibility determination: An authorized decision about qualification under the applicable criteria. / supporting evidence: Documents or information used to substantiate a claim. / verification: Checking information against appropriate evidence. / exception: A defined departure from a general requirement where permitted. / discretion: Authorized judgment within the limits of a rule or role. / statutory authority: Power granted by legislation. / rulemaking: The process of developing and issuing regulations. / public comment: Input submitted through an announced public consultation process. / comment deadline: The closing time for input under a specified process. / notice: An official communication about a process, action, or decision. / superseded version: A document version replaced by a later applicable version. / case-specific advice: Guidance tailored to a person's facts within the provider's authority.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-8 in pairs, stressing proposal, current, and publication date. Swap roles for turns 9-20, keeping an eligibility explanation separate from a formal decision. Complete the H3 exchange below and check every answer before reading it aloud.
Partner: Amina: Resident / Theo: Program information officer
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Guidance H3 states an eight-month residence condition. Proposal Q1 would reduce it to four months but is not adopted. Resident Jo has six months of residence. No formal application decision has been made.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Amina: Then I should keep the two documents separate and not rely on the proposed six-month period for the current requirements.
Theo: Exactly. Check the adoption status before treating a change as operative. Until then, use G4 for the current program explanation and P2 for the suggested change.
Amina: Thank you. Please show me where the current application steps are, and I will read the proposal notice separately.
Theo: I will. The application intake information will explain what to submit and how the review proceeds. Today's explanation neither guarantees an award nor substitutes for that formal process.
END REFERENCE

AI responses can contain mistakes. Compare feedback with the lesson and check specialist claims against the course references. Use these exercises for language practice; follow qualified guidance for actual professional decisions.

For teachers and study partners

Ask learners to respond before revealing the model. Give feedback on one meaning issue and one language pattern, then let them repeat. For mixed levels, offer the frames first and remove them in the second round.

Language notes and further reading

The cases and explanations are original teaching material. The references provide language frameworks and selected professional context; use current local guidance for actual work.
